  specified region leaves none, since its whole boundary meets $X$.
  As $X$ has no surviving black cut, the final black cut is strictly
  smaller than the number of added internal red edges. The same counting
  as in simultaneous restoration gives the size bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Positive witnesses from adjacent core pieces]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-core-pair-positive}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.RestorationFamily.exists_positive_of_core_adj,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.RestorationFamily.exists_positive_of_restricted_core_adj}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-closed-core-positive,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-core-forest}
  Let a restoration family have black degree at most $d$ and
  $|P_i\cup A_i|\le L$. After isolating a subfamily and deleting all
  remaining region boundaries, suppose each region is identified with
  a distinct connected component whose support is exactly that region.
  Form the associated core graph.
  If core pieces $C,D$ are adjacent, the original graph has a positive set
  $Y\supseteq C\cup D$ with
  \[
    |Y|\le (1+dL)(|C|+|D|).
  \]
  All incident regions, including previously isolated ones, are restored
  in this one bound.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Deleting all remaining region boundaries after partial isolation gives
  the same graph as deleting all original boundaries at once. Thus
  $C\cup D$ is closed in the fully deleted graph and avoids all regions.
  Distinct core pieces cannot have a direct edge in the boundary quotient,
  so their core edge passes through a region $P_i$. Its two connections
  to $C,D$ are distinct original edges. As $|\partial_B P_i|\le2$, these
  exhaust its nonempty boundary, which is absorbed by $C\cup D$.
  Apply the closed-core witness theorem.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Positive witnesses from light weighted core graphs]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-weighted-core-positive}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.RestorationFamily.exists_positive_of_light_core_forest,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.RestorationFamily.exists_positive_of_light_core_tree}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-core-pair-positive,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-weighted-tree-pair}
  Assign natural weights dominating core-piece sizes. If the core graph
  is a forest, every leaf and isolated vertex has weight at least $M$,
  and total weight is less than $M$ times the number of core vertices,
  there is a positive witness of size at most $2M(1+dL)$ in the original
  graph. For a nontrivial tree, it suffices that each leaf has weight at
  least $M$ and total weight is at most $M(n-1)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The weighted-forest or weighted-tree inequality gives an adjacent pair
  of total weight at most $2M$. The weights dominate the piece sizes;
  apply the adjacent-core witness theorem. For the actual thin family,
  $d=3$ and $L=3M$ give the bound $2M(1+9M)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Deleting designated edges to bridges]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-cycle-edge-selection}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.exists_delete_to_bridges}
  \leanok
  Let $T\subseteq E(B)$ be designated edges of a finite simple graph.
  There is $F\subseteq T$ such that $B-F$ has exactly the same
  reachability relation as $B$, every edge of $T\setminus F$ is a bridge
  in $B-F$, and
  \[
    |F|+|V|\le |E(B)|+c(B),
  \]
  where $c(B)$ is the number of connected components. Thus the number
  deleted is at most the original cycle rank.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Choose a largest subset of $T$ whose deletion preserves reachability.
  Any retained designated non-bridge could also be deleted, contradicting
  maximality. Each component of the remaining graph has at least one fewer
  edge than vertices. Sum over components, use the unchanged component
  count, and account for the $|F|$ deleted edges.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Reserving rank for untouched cyclic components]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-cycle-rank-reserve}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.card_vertices_add_cyclic_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.card_removed_add_untouched_cyclic_le}
  \leanok
  Let $F\subseteq E(B)$ have reachability-preserving deletion. Let
  $\mathcal C$ be any collection of original connected components,
  each containing a cycle and having all its internal edges retained.
  Then
  \[
    |F|+|\mathcal C|+|V|\le |E(B)|+c(B).
  \]
  In particular, the deleted edges and the untouched cyclic components
  share one cycle-rank budget.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  A connected graph has at least one fewer edge than vertices, with
  equality only for a tree. Each cyclic component therefore supplies
  one additional edge. Sum these inequalities over components using
  the degree-sum identity. Reachability-preserving deletion identifies
  the old and new components with equal supports, and untouched internal
  edges preserve each designated cycle. Account for the deleted edges.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Isolating regions to eliminate cycles through them]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-cycle-region-selection}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.cycle_contains_boundary_of_meets_region,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.exists_isolate_regions_without_cycles}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-cycle-edge-selection}
  Let $P_i$ be connected regions whose vertices have black degree at most
  two. Choose a boundary edge $e_i\in\partial_B P_i$ for each region,
  with all chosen edges distinct. Some set $J$ of at most the original
  cycle rank many indices satisfies both of the following: deleting only
  $\{e_i:i\in J\}$ preserves reachability, and deleting all cuts
  $\bigcup_{i\in J}\partial_B P_i$ leaves no cycle meeting any $P_i$.
  This selects regions for a supplied thin-path system. The construction
  below instantiates it. The later marked-tree reorganization is not needed
  by the connected-cluster proof of the sharp bisection theorem.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  At a degree-two vertex on a cycle, both incident edges belong to the
  cycle. Propagate this fact through a connected region: a cycle meeting
  it contains every boundary edge. Apply the preceding theorem to the
  chosen edges. A cycle in the resulting graph can contain none of them:
  each is deleted or is a bridge. It therefore avoids every region.
  Deleting the remaining boundary edges of the selected regions cannot
  create a cycle. Injectivity of the chosen edges identifies the number
  of selected regions with the number of selected edges.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Connectivity outside the isolated regions]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-isolation-core-connectivity}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.reachable_after_isolating}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-cycle-region-selection}
  Let $P_i$ have at most two black boundary edges, and choose one boundary
  edge $e_i$ for each selected index $i\in J$. Suppose deleting the chosen
  edges preserves reachability. Then deleting the full cuts of the selected
  regions preserves reachability between any two vertices outside
  $\bigcup_{i\in J}P_i$. Disjointness of the regions is not needed.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  After deleting the chosen edges, each selected region has at most one
  boundary edge left. A simple path between outside vertices cannot cross
  that boundary: entering and leaving would repeat its sole edge. Choose
  a simple path in the graph after the chosen-edge deletions. It avoids
  every remaining selected boundary and therefore survives full isolation.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[The component quotient across designated edges]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-bridge-quotient}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.BridgeQuotient.graph,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.BridgeQuotient.ofRegions}
  \leanok
  Delete designated edges $F$ and take the connected components of $B-F$
  as quotient vertices. Two distinct components are adjacent when an
  original black edge joins them. The region-boundary quotient designates
  the union of all black cuts of a supplied region family.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Faithful forest quotients across bridges]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-bridge-quotient}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.BridgeQuotient.reachable_project,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.BridgeQuotient.reachable_iff,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.BridgeQuotient.connected,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.BridgeQuotient.edge_image,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.BridgeQuotient.edge_map_injOn,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.BridgeQuotient.edge_ncard,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.BridgeQuotient.isAcyclic,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.BridgeQuotient.isTree,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.BridgeQuotient.component_isTree,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.BridgeQuotient.isBridge_of_mem_region_cut,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.BridgeQuotient.ofRegions_isAcyclic,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.BridgeQuotient.ofRegions_component_isTree}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-bridge-quotient}
  Quotient reachability is exactly original reachability between any
  representatives. If every designated actual edge is a bridge, the
  quotient is a forest and every component is a tree. The designated
  actual edges map bijectively to quotient edges: none becomes a loop
  and distinct edges do not merge. In particular, the quotient has
  $|F\cap E(B)|$ edges. If every cycle avoids a region family, its
  region-boundary quotient is a forest.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Project original walks, collapsing steps within a component; lift
  quotient walks using paths through each connected contracted piece.
  A quotient path avoiding the image of a designated bridge would lift
  to an original path avoiding that bridge, a contradiction. Thus every
  quotient edge is a bridge. The same lifting argument rules out a
  collapsed bridge or two bridges with the same quotient endpoints.
  Finally, every surviving boundary edge of a region avoided by all
  cycles is a bridge, so the general result applies to region boundaries.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Region vertices and their quotient degrees]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-quotient-region-vertices}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.BridgeQuotient.induce_delete_region_cuts,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.BridgeQuotient.exists_region_embedding,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.BridgeQuotient.ncard_neighborSet_le_cut,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.BridgeQuotient.ncard_neighborSet_eq_cut}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-bridge-quotient}
  Deleting any subset of the boundaries of pairwise disjoint regions
  preserves the induced graph inside each region. If the regions are
  connected, deleting all their boundaries makes each region exactly
  one connected component, hence one distinct vertex of the quotient.
  Every quotient vertex has degree at most the black cut of its support.
  If the designated actual edges are bridges, this bound is an equality.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  An edge internal to one region crosses no disjoint region. After all
  boundaries are deleted, internal connectivity and the empty cut identify
  the region with one component. Different regions give different components.
  For each neighbor of a quotient vertex, choose an original connecting
  edge. Distinct neighbors give distinct edges in the component's cut.
  Conversely, every boundary edge goes to another quotient vertex. When
  these edges are bridges, injectivity of the edge projection ensures
  that distinct boundary edges give distinct neighbors.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Path suppression and the core graph]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-path-suppression}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSuppression.graph,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSuppression.coreGraph}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-bridge-quotient}
  Suppress a vertex set $S$ by retaining vertices outside $S$, keeping
  their original edges, and adding an edge between distinct vertices
  joined by a two-edge path through $S$. The core graph suppresses the
  designated region vertices of the region-boundary quotient.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Suppressing path vertices preserves forests and connectivity]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-path-suppression}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSuppression.reachable_iff,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSuppression.connected,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSuppression.isAcyclic,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSuppression.isTree}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-path-suppression,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-bridge-quotient}
  Suppressing an independent set preserves reachability between surviving
  vertices. Suppressing vertices of degree at most two in a forest leaves
  a forest. Consequently, suppressing an independent set of such vertices
  in a tree leaves a tree whenever at least one vertex survives. Isolated
  vertices and leaves are allowed among the suppressed vertices.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Assign each removed vertex with a surviving neighbor to one such
  neighbor, and fix surviving vertices. Lift new edges to one- or two-edge
  original paths and project original walks through this assignment.
  Independence ensures reachability survives projection.
  Contract edges whose assigned endpoints agree. Distinct surviving
  vertices remain in distinct contracted components, and the degree-two
  bound makes each new edge an edge of this bridge quotient. Thus the
  suppressed graph embeds into a forest.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Counting edges after path suppression]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-path-suppression-count}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSuppression.exists_connection_equiv,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSuppression.edge_ncard_eq_degree_two}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-path-suppression}
  In a finite forest, suppose both $S$ and its complement are independent,
  and every vertex of $S$ has degree at most two. There is a bijection
  from the degree-two vertices of $S$ to the edges of the suppressed graph.
  The edge corresponding to a removed vertex joins its two original
  neighbors. Isolated vertices and leaves contribute no edge.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  A degree-two vertex has two distinct neighbors outside $S$.
  Two removed vertices cannot have the same neighbor pair: that would
  give two different simple paths between the same endpoints in a forest.
  Every surviving edge comes from such a pair, since there are no original
  edges inside the complement of $S$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The forest on the core pieces]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-core-forest}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSuppression.exists_core_forest}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-quotient-region-vertices,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-bridge-quotient,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-path-suppression,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-path-suppression-count}
  Let the regions be connected, pairwise disjoint, with no black edges
  between distinct regions, and with at most two boundary edges each.
  If every black cycle avoids the regions, identifying and suppressing
  their quotient vertices produces a forest on the remaining pieces.
  Reachability between these core pieces is exactly original reachability
  between their representative vertices.
  The number of core edges equals the number of regions with exactly
  two boundary edges.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The region vertices are independent in the boundary quotient and have
  degree at most two. The quotient is a forest because cycles avoid the
  regions. Apply path suppression, then compose its reachability equivalence
  with that of the component quotient.
  The complementary core vertices are also independent in the boundary
  quotient: each quotient edge crosses some region boundary. Exact
  quotient degrees and the preceding bijection give the edge count.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[The canonical thin-path system]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-thin-path-system}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.region,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.attachmentEdges,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.attachmentComponent,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.attachment,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.thinComponents}
  \leanok
  Given eligible vertices $U$, the regions are the connected components
  of $B[U]$, viewed as subsets of the original vertex set. For a region
  $P$, let $A_M(P)$ be the distinct red edges joining $P$ to an original
  black component of at most $M$ vertices. For each such edge choose its
  attached small component, preferring the second endpoint when both
  orientations qualify. A region is thin if $|P|\le M|A_M(P)|$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Paths and boundaries of the canonical regions]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-region-paths}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.region_subset,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.region_connected,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.exists_mem_region,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.region_disjoint,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.eq_of_adj,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.cut_disjoint,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.exists_distinct_boundary_edges,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.exists_spanning_path_in_region,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.region_spanning_path,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.card_cut_le_two,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.card_cut_eq_zero_or_two,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.region_isTree}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-thin-path-system,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-red-black-components}
  The regions partition $U$, with no black edges between distinct regions;
  their boundary cuts are pairwise disjoint. If every eligible vertex has
  black degree at most two, every region has a spanning simple path and
  at most two boundary edges. A region with nonempty boundary is a tree.
  If the ambient degrees are exactly two, its boundary size is zero or two.
  Every subfamily with nonempty cuts admits distinct choices of boundary edges.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Induced connected components give the partition and separation.
  A longest simple path in a finite connected graph of maximum degree two
  spans the graph: an external neighbor at an endpoint would extend it,
  and each internal vertex already has two path neighbors. Thus its support
  is closed under adjacency and connectivity forces it to be all vertices.
  Apply this to each induced region. Connectedness and the degree-sum
  identity give the boundary bound; a nonempty boundary forces exactly
  $|P|-1$ internal edges, while degree exactly two makes the cut size even.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Thin regions without supplied path orderings]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-thin-region-witness}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.exists_positive_of_thin_region,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.thin_component_bounds,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.card_cut_thin_eq_two}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-region-paths,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-red-black-thin-walk}
  A connected degree-two region $P$ with $m\ge3$ distinct red attachments
  to closed sets of size at most $M$, and $|P|\le Mm$, gives a positive
  set of at most $8M+1$ vertices. No walk or attachment ordering is assumed.
  Consequently, if no positive set of that size exists, every canonical
  thin region has one or two red attachments, at most $2M$ vertices, and
  a nonempty black boundary.
  With ambient degree exactly two, that boundary has exactly two edges.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Construct a spanning simple path, choose the position of each attachment,
  and sort the distinct red edges by these positions. Equal positions are
  allowed. The existing thin-walk theorem applies since the path length
  is $|P|-1$. Thinness and nonemptiness force at least one attachment;
  excluding three attachments bounds $|P|$ by $2M$. A closed thin region
  together with one small attachment would itself be a positive set of
  at most $3M$ vertices, so its boundary cannot be empty.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Attachment marks and the reserved cycle budget]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-thin-path-marks}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.attachmentMarks,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.SmallCycleBudget}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-thin-path-system}
  For each shaded thin path $P$ and each of its $\alpha(P)$ red
  attachments, give $3-\alpha(P)$ marks to the attached small component.
  Marks are a finite map to natural numbers, so repeated targets retain
  their multiplicities. A count $k$ satisfies the reserved cycle budget
  when, for every collection $\mathcal C$ of original cyclic components
  of size at most $M$,
  \[
    k+|\mathcal C|+|V|\le |E(B)|+c(B).
  \]
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Counting the initial attachment marks]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-thin-path-marks}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.attachmentMarks_apply,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.attachmentMarks_eq_zero_of_large,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.degree_attachmentMarks,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.card_doublyMarked_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.smallCycleBudget,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.smallCycleBudget_of_doublyMarked}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-thin-path-marks,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-thin-region-witness,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-cycle-rank-reserve}
  Suppose eligible vertices have degree at most two and there is no
  positive set of size at most $8M+1$. Shading a subfamily $J$ assigns
  exactly $2|J|$ attachment marks, all to original components of size
  at most $M$. Any collection $\mathcal D$ of components with at least
  two marks satisfies $|\mathcal D|\le |J|$.

  Suppose also that eligible vertices lie in original components larger
  than $M$, and deleting distinct designated boundary edges of $J$
  preserves reachability. Then $|J|$ satisfies the reserved cycle budget.
  Consequently, for any collection $\mathcal C$ of small cyclic components,
  \[
    |\mathcal D|+|\mathcal C|+|V|\le |E(B)|+c(B).
  \]
  This counts the initial shading step. Preservation through later color
  swaps and leaf reorganization is a separate obligation.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Each thin path has $\alpha(P)\in\{1,2\}$, and
  $\alpha(P)(3-\alpha(P))=2$. Sum the finite mark map; counting
  multiplicities makes the identity hold even when targets coincide.
  Each doubly marked component consumes at least two marks. A boundary
  edge of a thin region touches its large original component, so no such
  edge is internal to a small component. The cycle-rank reserve theorem
  applies to the designated deletions and every small cyclic component.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Constructing and isolating the thin-path family]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-constructed-thin-family}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.exists_isolated_family}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-restoration-family,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-thin-region-witness,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-cycle-region-selection,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-thin-path-marks,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-isolation-core-connectivity,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-bridge-quotient}
  Suppose every eligible vertex has black degree at most two and lies in
  an original black component larger than $M$. If no positive set of at
  most $8M+1$ vertices exists, the canonical thin regions form a restoration
  family with one chosen small attachment each, and
  $|P_i\cup A_i|\le3M$. Choose distinct boundary edges and isolate some
  subfamily $J$, with
  \[
    |J|+|V|\le |E(B)|+c(B).
  \]
  The stronger reserved cycle budget holds as well: each original small
  cyclic component reserves another unit in this inequality.
  Deleting its chosen single edges preserves reachability. Deleting its
  full cuts preserves reachability among outside vertices and leaves no
  cycle meeting any thin region. The resulting region-boundary quotient
  is a forest, with every connected component a tree. The simultaneous
  restoration theorem applies with $L=3M$, hence with factor $1+9M$ in
  a subcubic black graph for witnesses avoiding the isolated regions.
  The following theorem completes the suppression of the thin-path
  vertices. The connected-cluster argument below supplies the final density
  contradiction without requiring weighted-tree reorganization.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Choose one of the one or two attachments of each thin region. Large
  original components containing eligible vertices are disjoint from every
  chosen small component, giving all region--attachment separations.
  The region and attachment sizes are at most $2M$ and $M$ respectively.
  Disjoint cuts give injective boundary choices, so the preceding cycle
  selection theorem applies to this constructed restoration family.
  Apply the core-connectivity theorem to the full-cut deletion, and the
  bridge-quotient theorem to the surviving boundaries of all thin regions.
  The designated deletions leave small original components untouched,
  giving the stronger cycle budget from the preceding mark theorem.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The core forest of the constructed thin paths]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-constructed-core-forest}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.exists_core_forest}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-constructed-thin-family,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-isolation-core-connectivity,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-core-forest}
  Under the hypotheses of the constructed thin-family theorem, identify
  all thin regions in the boundary quotient after isolation and suppress
  their vertices. The resulting core graph is a forest, and reachability
  between its vertices agrees with reachability between their representatives
  in the original black graph. The $3M$ restoration bound per region and
  attachment and the original cycle-rank bound on isolated regions are retained.
  This includes the reserve for every original small cyclic component.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Isolation deletes no internal edge of any region, so the regions remain
  connected and separated and their boundaries still have size at most two.
  Apply the preceding core-forest construction. A core representative belongs
  to none of the thin regions, so the outside-connectivity theorem upgrades
  reachability in the graph after isolation to original black reachability.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Counting the surviving thin paths]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-constructed-core-count}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.RestorationFamily.cut_deletedGraph_restrict,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.exists_counted_core_forest}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-constructed-core-forest,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-path-suppression-count,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-thin-region-witness}
  Under the hypotheses of the constructed thin-family theorem, suppose
  eligible vertices have black degree exactly two. If $\mathcal P$ is the
  original thin-path family, $J$ is the selected shaded subfamily, and
  $T$ is the constructed core forest, then
  \[
    |E(T)|+|J|=|\mathcal P|.
  \]
  The same construction retains the restoration bound, original
  reachability between core representatives, and the cycle-rank bound
  $|J|+|V|\le |E(B)|+c(B)$.
  The stronger reserve for original small cyclic components is retained too.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  A thin region has a nonempty boundary. Since all its vertices have
  ambient degree two, that boundary has exactly two edges. The boundaries
  of distinct regions are disjoint. Isolating $J$ therefore empties
  precisely its cuts and preserves every other region's cut. The core
  edge count is consequently the number of unshaded regions. Partition
  the original thin-path family into its shaded and unshaded members.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Endpoint marks of the constructed thin paths]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-constructed-endpoint-marks}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.degree_endpointMarks,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.endpointMarks_eq_zero_of_mem,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.degree_isolated_add_endpointMarks,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.PathSystem.newly_degree_two_marked}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-constructed-thin-family,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-endpoint-marks,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-family-restoration}
  For the constructed restoration family and shaded subfamily $J$, endpoint
  marks vanish on every eligible vertex. If eligible vertices have ambient
  degree exactly two, their total is $2|J|$. At every ineligible vertex,
  remaining degree plus endpoint marks equals original degree. Consequently,
  a degree-three vertex made degree two by isolation receives exactly one mark.
  It cannot become a new unmarked degree-two path vertex.

  For a positive witness $X$ avoiding the shaded regions, the original graph
  has a positive witness $Y\supseteq X$ with
  \[
    |Y|\le |X|+3M\sum_{v\in X}m(v).
  \]
  These are the endpoint invariants of the initial shading step. Preservation
  through later color swaps and leaf reorganization is not used by the
  connected-cluster proof below.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Every thin region has two boundary edges, giving two outside marks.
  An eligible vertex adjacent to a vertex of a canonical region belongs
  to the same induced component. Thus no boundary edge can mark an eligible
  vertex. Apply the general degree identity outside the eligible set.
  A degree-three vertex was ineligible, and a drop to degree two gives one mark.
  The constructed family has region-plus-attachment size at most $3M$,
  so the mark-based restoration theorem gives the stated witness bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Bounded connected partitions]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-connected-clusters}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.ConnectedPartition.exists_partition}
  \leanok
  A connected graph of maximum degree three on $n>M>0$ vertices has a
  partition into $k$ connected sets, each of size at most $3M$, with
  $Mk\le 2n$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Recursively remove a root and partition the resulting components.
  Keep finished pieces of size between $M$ and $3M$ and a connected
  remainder of size less than $M$ containing the chosen root, or an empty
  remainder. There are at most three child components. Their remainders
  join through the root to a connected set of size at most $3M$; finish it
  if it reaches size $M$. At the end only one piece can have size below
  $M$, so $Mk\le n+M\le2n$. This partition argument and its application
  below were developed in this formalization.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[A bounded positive set from red density]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-cluster-positive}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.exists_positive_of_density,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.RedBlack.exists_positive_of_red_density}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-red-black,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-red-black-components,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-connected-clusters}
  Let $B$ be a simple black graph and $R$ a loopless red multigraph on
  the same $n$ vertices, with total red-plus-black degree three everywhere.
  If $M>0$ and $(M+4)n<2M|E(R)|$, there is a positive set of at most
  $3M(1+3M)$ vertices. In particular, red density greater than $1/2+\epsilon$
  suffices whenever $2\le\epsilon M$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Let $U$ be the union of black components of size at most $M$.
  A red edge inside $U$ gives a positive set of size at most $2M$.
  Otherwise partition its complement $W$ into connected clusters $P_i$
  as above, with $Mk\le2|W|$. Write $A_i$ for the red edges between
  $P_i$ and $U$. If $|A_i|\le|\partial_B P_i|$ for all $i$, connectivity
  gives $|\partial_B P_i|+2|P_i|\le\sum_{v\in P_i}\deg_B(v)+2$.
  Sum this, use the degree identity and count each red edge meeting $U$
  exactly once. It follows that $2|E(R)|\le|W|+2k$, contradicting the
  density assumption after multiplying by $M$.
  A surplus cluster, together with the original small black components
  reached by its red edges, is positive. Each attachment has empty black
  cut and at most $M$ vertices. There are at most $3|P_i|$ red attachments,
  so the union has size at most $3M(1+3M)$. Edge identities preserve
  parallel-edge multiplicity even when several attachments coincide.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Uniformly bounded helpful sets]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-bounded-helpful}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.exists_normalized_helpful_of_density,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.exists_bounded_helpful}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-cluster-positive,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-boundary-normalization,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-red-density,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-suppression}
  Let $\xi>0$, $M>0$, and $4\le3\xi M$. Every side $S$ in a simple cubic
  graph with $|\partial S|>(1/3+\xi)|S|$ contains a helpful set of at most
  $36M(1+3M)$ vertices. If the boundary is independent and each boundary
  vertex has exactly one outside neighbor, the bound is $12M(1+3M)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  For a normalized side, suppress its boundary to obtain red density
  greater than $1/2+3\xi/2$. The preceding theorem applies, and boundary
  lifting costs a factor four. For an arbitrary side, the first boundary
  normalization either supplies a helpful set of at most $33$ vertices
  or transfers the normalized witness back with a factor-three size bound.
  The second normalization phase and marked-tree reorganization are not
  needed by this argument.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Accumulating bounded helpful sets]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-helpful-amplification}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.exists_helpful_set_of_margin}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-cut-improvement}
  Let $a\ge 0$ and let $M,k$ be natural numbers. Suppose
  $|\partial S|>a|S|+k$, and every $T\subseteq S$ with
  $|\partial T|>a|T|$ contains a set of at most $M$ vertices of
  helpfulness at least one. Then some $X\subseteq S$ has
  $|X|\le kM$ and $h_S(X)\ge k$. This proves the accumulation step in
  Monien--Preis's argument. The preceding bounded helpful-set theorem
  supplies its local hypothesis uniformly.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Until the total helpfulness reaches $k$, the remaining side has cut
  size greater than $|\partial S|-k>a|S|$, hence greater than $a$
  times its own cardinality. Apply the local lemma, remove its helpful
  set, and add the gain. At most $k$ moves are needed, and their sets
  are disjoint. Each contributes at most $M$ vertices.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Prescribed-size subsets and logarithmic-cost rebalancing]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-rebalancing}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.exists_subset_cut_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.exists_rebalancing_set,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MedianOrdering.card_cutFinset_le_bag}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-subcubic-endpoint,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-cut-improvement}
  In a cubic graph, every side $S$ and $0\le r\le |S|$ admit a subset
  $T\subseteq S$ of size $r$ with
  \[
    |\partial T|\le
    \max\{ |\partial S|,\lfloor |S|/3\rfloor+1\}
      +\lceil\log_2|S|\rceil+2.
  \]
  Consequently, if $|S|<3|\partial S|$, every prescribed move size
  $k\le |S|$ has a witness $X\subseteq S$ with
  $h_S(X)\ge-\lceil\log_2|S|\rceil-2$.
  No bisection hypothesis or connectivity assumption is used.
  The ceiling logarithm is interpreted as zero at zero.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Delete vertices with at least two outside neighbors while the side is
  larger than the requested subset; each deletion decreases the cut.
  Once each remaining vertex has two internal neighbors, take its induced
  graph's prescribed-endpoint decomposition ending at the cut boundary.
  Extend it by one bag containing that boundary and the other side.
  Place edges in their first available bags. Every vertex on the chosen
  side has its median edge before the final bag; every other vertex has
  its median in the final bag. An initial segment of any prescribed
  cardinality is therefore contained in the chosen side. The median
  charging argument bounds its cut by one more than an earlier bag.
  For the move, take $X=S\setminus T$ with $|T|=|S|-k$ and use the exact
  helpfulness identity. The density condition absorbs the maximum.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Sharp bisection from bounded helpful sets]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-helpful-reduction}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.exists_bisection_of_helpful,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.exists_bisectionBound_of_helpful}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-helpful-amplification,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-rebalancing,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-bisection}
  Fix $\xi>0$. Suppose every side with cut density greater than
  $1/3+\xi$ contains a set of at most $M$ vertices of helpfulness at
  least one. A cubic graph on $n$ vertices has a bisection of size at most
  $(1/6+\xi)n$ whenever
  \[
    (M+3)(\lceil\log_2 n\rceil+4)<\xi n.
  \]
  A graph-independent $M$ therefore gives
  $\mathrm{BisectionBound}(\xi,N_0)$ for some $N_0$.
  The preceding bounded helpful-set theorem supplies a uniform $M$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Start from a minimum cut with one side of size $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$.
  If its size exceeds the bound, accumulate helpfulness
  $k=\lceil\log_2 n\rceil+3$ using at most $kM$ vertices.
  From the enlarged opposite side, select a subset of its original
  cardinality using the preceding theorem. The original-cut term of
  its maximum is paid by the accumulated gain; the side-size term is
  below the original cut by the displayed size condition. This restores
  balance with a strictly smaller cut, contradicting minimality.
  The maximum also handles overshoot of the accumulated gain.
  Finally, a fixed multiple of the logarithm is sublinear.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Sharp cubic bisection]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-sharp-bisection}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Bisection.exists_bisectionBound}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-bounded-helpful,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-helpful-reduction}
  For every $\xi>0$ there is $N_0$ such that every simple cubic graph on
  $n>N_0$ vertices has a balanced cut of at most $(1/6+\xi)n$ edges.
  This is the Monien--Preis bisection conclusion, with the connected-cluster
  argument replacing the red/black reorganization in their proof.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Choose an integer $M>4/(3\xi)$. The helpful-set bound
  $36M(1+3M)$ depends only on $\xi$. Apply the preceding local-to-global
  reduction and absorb its logarithmic remainder for large $n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[A coarse bisection bound]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-bisection-coarse}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.bisectionBound_coarse}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-bisection}
  The predicate $\mathrm{BisectionBound}(4/3,0)$ holds. This checks the
  bisection contract with a coarse constant and does not prove the sharp
  theorem for arbitrarily small positive slack.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Choose any $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$ vertices. The cut has at most all
  $3n/2$ edges of the cubic graph, by the degree-sum formula.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Path decomposition from a balanced cut]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-bisection-assembly}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.PathDecomposition.exists_of_cut,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.PathDecomposition.exists_of_balanced_cut,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.card_cutBoundary_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.card_cutFinset_induce_le}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-subcubic-endpoint,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-boundary-transition,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-endpoint-glue}
  If a graph of maximum degree three on $n$ vertices has a balanced cut
  with $b$ crossing edges, it has a path decomposition with bags of size
  at most
  \[
    \max\{b,\lfloor(n+1)/6\rfloor+1\}+\lceil\log_2 n\rceil+1.
  \]
  For an arbitrary cut, replace $\lfloor(n+1)/6\rfloor$ by
  $\lfloor\max\{|S|,|S^c|\}/3\rfloor$. The logarithmic term is zero
  for $n=0$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Each vertex boundary has at most $b$ vertices. Apply the prescribed
  endpoint theorem to both induced sides and reverse the second
  decomposition. Between them put the decomposition on the two vertex
  boundaries, with bags of size at most $b+1$. The adjoining bags contain
  all shared vertices, so concatenation preserves the bag-size bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Cubic pathwidth from bisection]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-bisection-pathwidth}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.BisectionBound.exists_pathwidthBound,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.pathwidthBound_of_bisectionBound,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.exists_pathwidthBound}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-bisection,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-bisection-assembly,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-sharp-bisection}
  If $\xi\ge 0$, $\delta>0$, and $\mathrm{BisectionBound}(\xi,N_0)$
  holds, then $\mathrm{PathwidthBound}(\xi+\delta,N_1)$ holds for some
  $N_1$. Consequently the sharp asymptotic cubic bisection theorem implies
  the sharp asymptotic cubic pathwidth theorem. This proves the reduction
  of Fomin and H{\o}ie. Instantiating the proved sharp bisection theorem
  gives $\mathrm{PathwidthBound}(\xi,N_0)$ for some $N_0$ at every $\xi>0$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  For all sufficiently large $n$, the ceiling logarithm plus two is less
  than $\delta n$. Absorb the finite assembly's remainder in that slack.
  For the universally quantified conclusion, allocate half the target
  slack to the bisection and half to the remainder.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Flat-source sumset extraction]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-sumset-extractor}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.FlatSumsetExtractor,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.flatSumsetExtractor_singleBit}
  \leanok
  A Boolean function $f$ on $n$ bits is a flat-source sumset extractor with
  threshold $K$ and error $\nu$ when, for all sets $P,Q\subseteq\{0,1\}^n$
  of cardinality at least $K$, the fraction of pairs $(x,y)\in P\times Q$
  with $f(x\oplus y)=1$ lies in $[1/2-\nu,1/2+\nu]$. Source pairs are
  counted with multiplicity even when their sums coincide. On one bit, the
  identity function has threshold $2$ and error zero. This finite example
  does not give an asymptotic family.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Sumset extraction supplies the hard-family properties]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-extractor-hardness}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.FlatSumsetExtractor.balanced,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.eventually_hard_of_flatSumsetExtractor}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-sumset-extractor}
  Suppose $\nu\le 1/4$, $\log_2 K(n)=o(n)$, and eventually $f_n$ is a
  flat-source sumset extractor with threshold $K(n)$ and error $\nu$.
  Then eventually $f_n$ is $K(n)$-rectangle-free and accepts at least
  $2^{n-2}$ inputs. More generally, flat-source extraction implies balance
  on all coordinate rectangles at the same threshold and error.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Zero-pad the two rectangle sides on complementary coordinates; XOR
  recovers their glued input, with each source pair retained. An all-one
  rectangle contradicts error below $1/2$. Apply balance to the full cube,
  splitting its coordinates into nearly equal halves, for the density bound.
  Sublinear source entropy ensures that both halves support enough inputs.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Two-sided sumset dispersion and balanced padding]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-extractor-padding}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.FlatSumsetDisperser,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.balancePad,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.balancePadEval}
  \leanok
  A function is a two-sided flat sumset disperser at threshold $K$ if
  both Boolean values occur among the sums of every pair of sources
  of size at least $K$. Balanced padding is the $(n+1)$-bit function
  $g(b,x)=b\oplus f(x)$, evaluated on lists by the corresponding tail
  evaluator followed by XOR with the first bit.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Balanced padding of an extractor]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-extractor-padding}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.FlatSumsetExtractor.disperser,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.FlatSumsetDisperser.balancePad,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.FlatSumsetDisperser.rectangleFree,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.FlatSumsetExtractor.balancePad_rectangleFree,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.card_accepting_balancePad,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.balancePadEval_toList,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.balancePadEval_mem_FP}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-extractor-padding,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-sumset-extractor}
  If $K>0$ and $f$ extracts with error below $1/2$, balanced padding is
  rectangle-free at threshold $2K$ and accepts exactly $2^n$ strings.
  Its uniform list evaluator agrees with the finite function and belongs
  to $\mathrm{FP}$ whenever the original evaluator does.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Error below $1/2$ forces both outputs on every large source pair.
  In each source of size at least $2K$, fix a first bit whose fiber has
  size at least $K$. Dispersion in the tails then supplies either padded
  outcome. Zero-padding complementary rectangle sides proves rectangle
  freeness. For each old input exactly one fresh bit accepts. The evaluator
  uses polynomial-time tail extraction, the given evaluator, and XOR.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Constant-error extraction gives padded circuit hardness]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-padded-hardness}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.eventually_lt_size_balancePad_of_flatSumsetExtractor,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.nondet_eventually_lt_size_balancePad_of_flatSumsetExtractor}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-extractor-padding,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-cutwidth-subexponential}
  Fix a family of flat sumset extractors with error $\nu<1/2$, eventually
  positive thresholds $K(n)$, and $\log_2 K(n)=o(n)$. For every
  $\varepsilon>0$ and all large $n$, its balanced padding requires more
  than $(4-\varepsilon)(n+1)$ gates, including for nondeterministic circuits
  with arbitrarily many witness inputs. The family is fixed before
  $\varepsilon$; an explicit family remains to be constructed.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  At length $n+1$ use threshold $2K(n)$. Doubling the threshold and shifting
  its index preserve sublinearity of the binary logarithm. Exact balance
  gives the accepting-input bound, so apply the graph-free circuit theorem
  to the single padded family at every positive length.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Majority evaluation and good-coordinate margin]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-extractor-majority}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.majorityEval,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.voteMargin}
  \leanok
  Strict majority returns false on ties, including the empty input.
  The margin on designated good coordinates is their number of true bits
  minus their number of false bits.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Polynomial-time majority and arbitrary bad coordinates]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-extractor-majority}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.majorityEval_toList,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.majorityEval_mem_FP,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.majority_eq_true_of_margin,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.majority_eq_false_of_margin}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-extractor-majority}
  The list majority evaluator agrees with finite-vector majority and is
  in $\mathrm{FP}$. A good-coordinate margin strictly greater than the
  number of bad coordinates forces true; a margin at most the negative
  of that number forces false. The bad coordinates may depend arbitrarily
  on the good ones.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Count true bits with the polynomial-time bounded-counting API.
  The bad coordinates change the margin by at most their number, and the
  evaluator uses the strict sign test.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Low-order parity bias]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-parity-bias}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.weightedMean,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.signSum,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.ParityBiasBound}
  \leanok
  For a finite weighted sample space and $m$ coordinates $\sigma_i$,
  write $S=\sum_i\sigma_i$. The parity-bias bound $\delta$ requires
  $|\mathbb E\prod_{i\in T}\sigma_i|\le\delta$ for every nonempty
  coordinate subset $T$ of size at most four. The coordinates being
  signs and the weights being probabilities are separate hypotheses.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Raw sign-sum moments from parity bias]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-parity-moments}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.signSum_first_moment,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.signSum_second_moment,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.signSum_third_moment,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.signSum_fourth_moment}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-parity-bias}
  On a finite probability space let $\sigma_1,\ldots,\sigma_m$ take
  values in $\{-1,1\}$ and satisfy the parity-bias bound $\delta\ge0$.
  Then
  \[
    |\mathbb E S|\le m\delta,\qquad
    |\mathbb E S^2-m|\le m^2\delta,\qquad
    |\mathbb E S^3|\le m^3\delta,\qquad
    \mathbb E S^4\le3m^2+m^4\delta.
  \]
  These are algebraic consequences of low-order parity bias. Producing
  that bias from an actual sumset source remains a construction obligation.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Expand each power into ordered coordinate tuples and cancel repeated
  signs in pairs. Odd tuples leave a nonempty parity. In degree two only
  the $m$ diagonal terms survive without a bias factor. In degree four
  the three possible pairings account for at most $3m^2$ such terms;
  every other tuple is bounded by $\delta$. Sum the bounds.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[A fourth-moment certificate for both tails]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-extractor-fourth-moment}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.fourthMoment_positive_tail_of_approx,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.fourthMoment_negative_tail_of_approx,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.fourthMoment_positive_tail,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.fourthMoment_negative_tail}
  \leanok
  On a finite probability space, suppose
  $|\mathbb E Z|,|\mathbb E Z^3|\le1/100$,
  $\mathbb E Z^2\ge99/100$, and $\mathbb E Z^4\le301/100$.
  Each of the events $Z>1/8$ and $Z<-1/8$ has probability at least $1/36$.
  In particular the conclusion holds when the first and third moments
  vanish, the second is one, and the fourth is at most three.
  This is a finite analytic component of the Chattopadhyay--Liao
  constant-error route; the source reduction and extractor primitives
  remain separate construction obligations.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The polynomial $q(u)=u(u+1)^2(3-u)$ is at most
  $36\mathbf1_{u>0}$. Expand $\mathbb E q(Z-1/8)$ and use the moment
  bounds to obtain a value greater than one. Apply the same argument to
  $-Z$ for the other tail. This quartic certificate is a direct calculation.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Parity bias forces both sign-sum tails]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-parity-tails}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.signSum_positive_tail_of_parityBias,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.signSum_negative_tail_of_parityBias}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-parity-moments,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-extractor-fourth-moment}
  On a finite probability space, let $m>0$ signs satisfy the parity-bias
  bound $\delta\ge0$, with $100m^2\delta\le1$. Each of the events
  $S>\sqrt m/8$ and $S<-\sqrt m/8$ has probability at least $1/36$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Normalize by $\sqrt m$. The raw moment bounds and the bias budget give
  the four hypotheses of the quartic tail certificate. Rescale its events.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Majority probabilities under parity bias]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-majority-probability}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.majority_mass_ge_of_parityBias}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-parity-tails,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-extractor-majority}
  In a finite probability distribution on $N$ bits, select $m>0$ good
  coordinates. Encode true as $1$ and false as $-1$. If their parity bias
  is at most $\delta\ge0$, $100m^2\delta\le1$, and
  $N-m\le\sqrt m/8$, each strict-majority outcome has probability at
  least $1/36$. The other coordinates may depend arbitrarily on the good
  coordinates, and ties return false.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  On each sign-sum tail the good-coordinate margin dominates all bad
  votes. Compare its event with the corresponding majority outcome.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Sumset extraction by averaging good fixings]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-extractor-conditioning}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.FlatSumsetExtractor.of_many_good_fibers,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.FlatSumsetExtractor.of_half_good_fibers}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-sumset-extractor}
  Let $K>0$ and $\rho\ge0$. For every $P,Q$ of size at least $K$,
  suppose a subset $G\subseteq Q$ has size at least $\theta|Q|$, and
  for every $y\in G$, both values of $f(X\oplus y)$ have probability
  at least $\rho$ when $X$ is uniform on $P$. Then $f$ is a flat
  sumset extractor with error $1/2-\rho\theta$. In particular,
  $\rho=1/36$ and $\theta=1/2$ give error $35/72$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Sum each conditional lower bound over $G$ and then over $Q$.
  The two outcome counts partition $P\times Q$, giving both sides
  of the extraction interval. Keep all pairs when their XORs coincide.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The parity target sufficient for sumset extraction]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-extractor-parity-target}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.majority_flatSumsetExtractor_of_parity_fibers}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-majority-probability,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-extractor-conditioning}
  Fix a map $R:\{0,1\}^n\to\{0,1\}^N$, a threshold $K>0$,
  and $\delta\ge0$. For every $P,Q$ of size at least $K$, suppose
  at least half the $y\in Q$ admit $m>0$ good coordinates of
  $R(X\oplus y)$, for uniform $X\in P$, satisfying the parity and
  size bounds of Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-majority-probability}.
  Then $\operatorname{Maj}\circ R$ is a flat sumset extractor with
  error $35/72<1/2$. The selected coordinates may depend on $P,Q,y$;
  $R$ is fixed before the source sets. This proves the final analytic
  implication, leaving construction and uniform evaluation of such an $R$
  as separate obligations.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Apply the majority probability bound on each good fixing, then the
  good-fixing averaging theorem with $\rho=1/36$ and $\theta=1/2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Finite seeded extraction and weak-source sampling]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-extractor-sampler}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.FlatSeededExtractor,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.Sampler,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.seedHits,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.badInputs}
  \leanok
  For finite seed and output sets, $E$ is a flat seeded extractor at
  threshold $K$ and error $\epsilon$ if every uniform source of support
  at least $K$, with an independent uniform seed, has output test-set
  discrepancy at most $\epsilon$. For each test of density at most
  $\epsilon$, an input is bad if more than a $2\epsilon$ fraction of
  seeds hit that test. The sampler contract requires bad-input probability
  at most $\delta$ for every source with point masses at most $1/L$.
  The definition records this cap as $Lp(x)\le1$; its entropy
  interpretation requires $L>0$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Seeded extraction supplies weak-source sampling]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-extractor-sampler}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.FlatSeededExtractor.card_badInputs_lt,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.FlatSeededExtractor.sampler,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.FlatSeededExtractor.card_badInputs_inter_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sampler_seedProjection}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-extractor-sampler}
  Assume the seed and output sets are nonempty. An extractor with
  threshold $K>0$ has fewer than $K$ bad inputs for each small test.
  If $L>0$ and $K\le\delta L$, it therefore satisfies the sampler
  contract, including for nonuniform source weights. For a uniform
  source on $P$, $K\le\delta|P|$ gives the direct bad-cardinality bound.
  Returning the seed itself is a sampler with zero failures for
  $\epsilon\ge0$, although its seed is as large as its output.

  This is the conversion credited to Zuckerman in Chattopadhyay--Liao
  Lemma 3.15. The finite budget gives sampler entropy
  $k+\log_2(1/\delta)$ from extractor entropy $k$, as used in their
  Lemma 3.18, correcting the reversed direction printed in Lemma 3.15.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  If at least $K$ inputs are bad, apply extraction to their uniform
  distribution. Its hit frequency is strictly greater than $2\epsilon$,
  contradicting the test-density bound plus extractor error. The source
  point-mass cap bounds their total probability by $K/L$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Neighbor coverage and somewhere sampling]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-sampler-amplification}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.neighborSet,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.NeighborCoverage,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.allHitSeeds,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.somewhereBadInputs,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.SomewhereSampler}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-extractor-sampler}
  A neighbor map sends each outer seed to a list of base seeds. Coverage
  $(K,\rho)$ means that every outer support of size at least $K$ reaches
  at least a $\rho$ fraction of base seeds. A somewhere sampler has a
  candidate list at each outer seed: the small-test failure event occurs
  when more than a $2\epsilon$ fraction of outer seeds have every
  candidate output in the test. It has probability at most $\delta$
  for every source with point masses bounded by $1/L$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Finite sampler amplification]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-sampler-amplification}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.NeighborCoverage.card_allHitSeeds_lt,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.FlatSeededExtractor.neighborCoverage,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.Sampler.amplify,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.neighborCoverage_seedProjection,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.somewhereSampler_seedProjection}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-sampler-amplification,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-extractor-sampler}
  Suppose $E$ is a sampler with parameters $(L,\epsilon_0,\delta)$
  and $\Gamma$ has coverage $(K,\rho)$ on a nonempty base seed set.
  If $\epsilon\le\epsilon_0$, $2\epsilon_0<\rho$, and
  $K\le2\epsilon A$ for $A$ outer seeds, then
  $S(x,y,z)=E(x,\Gamma(y,z))$ is a somewhere sampler with parameters
  $(L,\epsilon,\delta)$. A flat seeded extractor of error $e$ and
  positive threshold $K$ supplies coverage $(K,1-e)$ when its candidate
  seed set is nonempty. Listing every seed gives concrete full-coverage
  and zero-failure examples, without giving the short-seed parameters
  required by the eventual construction.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  At a base-good input the hit-seed set has size at most
  $2\epsilon_0$ times the base size. If $K$ outer seeds had all
  candidates in that set, coverage and $2\epsilon_0<\rho$ would
  contradict that size bound. Thus amplified bad inputs are base-bad;
  compare their probability sums. For extraction-to-coverage, test the
  full neighbor image, which receives every source--seed pair.
  The amplification argument follows Chattopadhyay--Liao Appendix A.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Low-order tests and their common bad-seed set]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-parity-bad-seeds}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.parityTests,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.parityBadSeeds}
  \leanok
  The parity tests are all nonempty subsets of the coordinate set of
  size at most $t$. Given a bad-seed set for each test and each candidate
  position, the common bad-seed set is their union over both indices.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Counting the common parity bad-seed set]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-parity-bad-seeds}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.card_parityTests_le_pow,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.card_parityBadSeeds_le}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-parity-bad-seeds}
  Among $A$ coordinates there are at most $A^t$ parity tests of order
  at most $t$. If each test and each of $C$ candidates excludes at most
  an $\eta\ge0$ fraction of seeds, their union excludes at most an
  $A^tC\eta$ fraction. Both bounds include empty coordinate, candidate,
  or seed types and order zero.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Each nonempty subset of size at most $t$ is the range of a $t$-tuple:
  enumerate the set and repeat a member in unused positions. Count all
  tuples, then apply a finite union bound over tests and over candidates.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Simultaneous parity-good fixings from a somewhere sampler]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-parity-selection}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.SomewhereSampler.exists_good_fibers,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.SomewhereSampler.parity_fibers}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-parity-bad-seeds,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-sampler-amplification,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-parity-bias}
  Let $S$ be a somewhere sampler with parameters $(L,\epsilon,\delta)$
  and $N$ outer coordinates. Fix a nonempty uniform source support $Q$
  with $L\le|Q|$. For every parity test of orders one through four
  and every candidate, suppose a seed set of density at most $\eta\ge0$
  is excluded, with $N^4C\eta\le\epsilon$.
  For each parity test suppose some member coordinate has this property:
  every candidate escaping its excluded set guarantees absolute weighted
  parity expectation at most $\beta$, for every fixing in $Q$.

  Then at least a $1-\delta$ fraction of fixings in $Q$ admit an
  embedding of $m$ good coordinates with $N-m\le2\epsilon N$ and
  parity-bias bound $\beta$. This selection theorem places no sign or
  probability assumptions on the first-source weights or coordinates;
  those remain hypotheses of the majority theorem. In the intended
  application the still-unproved correlation-breaker estimate and sampler
  linearity must supply the stated seed-escape implication.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Apply the sampler to the common bad-seed set and uniform weights on
  $Q$. At each good fixing retain every coordinate with an escaping
  candidate. For any parity on these coordinates, its distinguished
  member has such a candidate, which escapes its own test as well.
  Apply its estimate and reindex through the fixed embedding.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Modular polynomial map for the linear condenser]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-polynomial-condenser}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.modularFrobenius,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.polynomialCondenser}
  \leanok
  For a supplied polynomial $E$, set $g_0=f\bmod E$ and
  $g_{j+1}=g_j^2\bmod E$. For exponent $h=2^r$, output coordinates
  $g_{ri}(y)$ for $0\le i<m$; the seed $y$ is kept separate.
  This is the polynomial map of Guruswami--Umans--Vadhan with the
  characteristic-power exponent used by Cheraghchi for source linearity.
  It is an algebraic specification using Mathlib's polynomial type;
  an encoded evaluator and uniform field and modulus construction
  are separate obligations.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Polynomial-map formula, degree invariant, and linearity]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-polynomial-condenser}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.modularFrobenius_eq_pow_modByMonic,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.modularFrobenius_degree_lt,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.polynomialCondenser_apply,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.polynomialCondenser_zero_coordinate,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.polynomialCondenser_zero,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.polynomialCondenser_add,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.polynomialCondenser_smul}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-polynomial-condenser}
  Coordinate $i$ equals $(f^{h^i}\bmod E)(y)$. Over a nontrivial
  commutative ring, every stored polynomial has degree below that of
  monic $E$. For sources already below that degree, coordinate zero
  equals $f(y)$. In characteristic two the output map preserves zero,
  addition, and scalar multiplication over $\mathbb F_2$ for every
  fixed seed. No irreducibility hypothesis is needed for these algebraic
  facts. The finite expansion theorem below additionally requires an
  irreducible modulus; the full encoded construction is separate.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Induct over modular squaring and use remainder compatibility with
  multiplication for the power formula. Monic remainder has smaller
  degree. Squaring is additive in characteristic two, and remainder
  and evaluation preserve addition. An additive map between
  $\mathbb F_2$ modules respects their scalar action.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[The first monomial features and their substitution]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-monomials}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.monomialDigit,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.digitMonomial,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.substitutedSum}
  \leanok
  Let $e_i(j)=\lfloor j/h^i\rfloor\bmod h$ and
  $M_j(z)=\prod_{i<m}z_i^{e_i(j)}$. For $K$ coefficient polynomials
  $p_j$ define
  \[
    Q_f(Y)=\sum_{j<K}p_j(Y)M_j\bigl((f^{h^i}\bmod E)(Y)\bigr)_{i<m}.
  \]
  Only the first $K$ features are used, where $K$ will be the actual
  source cardinality, rather than the full capacity $h^m$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Digit substitution and degree bounds]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-monomials}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sum_monomialDigit_mul_pow,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sum_monomialDigit_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.digitMonomial_pow,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.digitMonomial_substitution,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.fin_monomial_sum_degree_lt,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.fin_monomial_sum_ne_zero_iff,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.substitutedSum_eval,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.substitutedSum_natDegree_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.substitutedSum_degree_lt}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-monomials}
  If $j<h^m$, then $M_j(u,u^h,\ldots,u^{h^{m-1}})=u^j$.
  Thus the first $K\le h^m$ features become a polynomial of degree
  below $K$, nonzero whenever some coefficient is nonzero. For monic
  $E$, $h>0$, $A>0$, and $\deg p_j<A$, the substituted sum satisfies
  \[
    \deg Q_f<A+(\deg E-1)(h-1)m.
  \]
  Evaluation commutes with the displayed substitution. No source-degree
  or irreducibility premise is needed for this degree estimate.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Base-$h$ digits reconstruct $j$ and have total at most $m(h-1)$.
  Each reduced coordinate has natural degree at most $\deg E-1$.
  Apply product, power, and sum degree bounds.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Interpolation with the actual feature count]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-interpolation}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.exists_polynomial_interpolant}
  \leanok
  For a field $F$, a finite set $T$, seed values $y_x\in F$, and
  weights $w_{x,j}\in F$, if $|T|<AK$ there is a nonzero family
  $(p_j)_{j<K}$ of polynomials of degree below $A$ satisfying
  $\sum_{j<K}p_j(y_x)w_{x,j}=0$ for every $x\in T$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The linear map from $AK$ coefficients to the $|T|$ constraints
  has a nontrivial kernel. Grouping coefficients into $K$ polynomials
  is injective, so the resulting family is nonzero.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Primitive coefficients and quotient-field root count]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-quotient}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.exists_primitive_polynomialFamily,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.substitutedSum_quotient_eval,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.card_lt_of_quotient_polynomial_roots}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-monomials,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-interpolation}
  If a positive-degree monic $E$ does not vanish at the constrained
  seeds, an interpolating family can be chosen with some coefficient
  not divisible by $E$, preserving its degree bound and constraints.
  For irreducible $E$, the polynomial
  $\sum_{j<K}[p_j]Z^j$ over $F[Y]/(E)$ is then nonzero of degree
  below $K$. Consequently fewer than $K$ distinct source polynomials
  of degree below $\deg E$ can give roots of this quotient polynomial.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Divide common factors of $E$ out of the coefficient family; each
  division strictly lowers its common degree bound. Cancel $E(y_x)$
  in each constraint. In the quotient, digit substitution gives the
  stated polynomial, while distinct bounded-degree representatives
  have distinct classes. Apply the field root bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[The polynomial neighbor graph]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-neighbors}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.polynomialNeighbor,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.polynomialNeighborSet}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-polynomial-condenser}
  The full neighbor of a source $f$ at seed $y\in F$ is
  $(y,((f^{h^i}\bmod E)(y))_{i<m})$. The neighbors of $P$ are the
  union of these pairs over $f\in P$ and every seed in $F$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Finite GUV polynomial expansion]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-expansion}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.le_card_polynomialNeighborSet,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.polynomialNeighborSet_expansion,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.card_polynomialNeighborSet_of_degree_one,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.polynomialNeighbor_pow_two}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-neighbors,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-quotient}
  Let $F$ have cardinality $q$, let $E$ be monic and irreducible of
  degree $n\ge2$, and let $h>0$. Every set $P$ of polynomials of
  degree below $n$, with $|P|\le h^m$, satisfies
  \[
    |\Gamma(P)|\ge\bigl(q-(n-1)(h-1)m\bigr)|P|,
  \]
  where subtraction is truncated at zero. This is the finite
  expansion argument of Guruswami--Umans--Vadhan (2009), Theorem 3.3.
  For degree-one moduli and at least one output coordinate, the exact
  neighbor count is $q|P|$. For $h=2^r$, the full neighbor equals the
  seed paired with the modular-squaring condenser output.
  The field and modulus are supplied; this theorem alone makes no
  uniform construction or distributional claim.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Put $K=|P|$ and $A=q-(n-1)(h-1)m$. If expansion fails and $A>0$,
  interpolate with exactly $K$ features against the neighbor union.
  Primitive normalization is possible because $E$ has no roots in $F$.
  Each substituted polynomial has degree below $q$ and vanishes at all
  $q$ seeds, hence is zero. The quotient-field root count would then
  give $|P|<K$, a contradiction. If $A=0$, the bound is immediate.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Seeded neighbors and uniform test probabilities]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-flat-lossless}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.seededNeighborSet,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.seededTestProb}
  \leanok
  For a finite source support $P$ and seeded map $C$, let
  $\Gamma_C(P)=\{(y,C(x,y)):x\in P,\ y\in S\}$. The seeded test
  probability is the fraction of independent uniform source and seed
  pairs whose joint seed-output belongs to the test set. Repeated
  outputs retain their multiplicity in this probability.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Seed-preserving flat lossless condensation]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-flat-lossless}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.exists_seedwise_injection,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.seededTestProb_sub_le_disagreement,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.exists_seedwise_injection_of_expansion,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.polynomialCondenser_flat_lossless}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-flat-lossless,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-expansion}
  Suppose $P,S$ are nonempty, the output alphabet has at least $|P|$
  elements, and $|\Gamma_C(P)|\ge(1-\epsilon)|P||S|$. There are
  injections $g_y:P\hookrightarrow\Omega$, one for each seed,
  such that every test on $(y,C(x,y))$ differs in probability by at
  most $\epsilon$ from the same test on $(y,g_y(x))$.
  Conditional on each seed, this ideal output is uniform on exactly
  $|P|$ values. The construction preserves exactly $|\Gamma_C(P)|$
  original source-seed pairs.

  For the polynomial map with $h=2^r\le q$, supplied irreducible
  modulus of degree $n\ge2$, and a nonempty qualifying source set
  $|P|\le h^m$, the error is $(n-1)(h-1)m/q$. The source-dependent
  witness is a distributional comparison, not an evaluator or a
  theorem about arbitrary weighted sources.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  For each seed retain one actual source representative for every
  reached output. Extend this partial assignment to an injection using
  the alphabet capacity. Agreeing pairs map bijectively to reached
  neighbors. Every change in a test outcome occurs on a disagreeing
  pair, so division by $|P||S|$ gives the bound. Apply finite polynomial
  expansion, including its valid trivial bound when the loss exceeds $q$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[A sparse binomial modulus family]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-binomial-modulus}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binomialModulus}
  \leanok
  For a coefficient $a$ in the supplied field, set
  $E_{a,s}(X)=X^{3^s}-a$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Noncubes yield irreducible moduli at rounded degrees]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-binomial-modulus}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binomialModulus_monic,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binomialModulus_natDegree,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binomialModulus_irreducible,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.exists_noncube,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.three_dvd_card_sub_one_of_even_exponent,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binomialModulus_natDegree_clog_bounds}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-binomial-modulus}
  If $a$ is not a cube, every $E_{a,s}$ is monic and irreducible,
  with degree exactly $3^s$, including $s=0$. A noncube exists in any
  finite field with $3\mid(q-1)$, in particular when $q=2^b$ and $b$
  is even. For every $n>0$, choosing $s=\lceil\log_3 n\rceil$
  gives $n\le\deg E_{a,s}<3n$. These are semantic statements;
  they do not provide encoded field or coefficient selection.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Reuse Mathlib's odd-prime-power binomial irreducibility theorem in
  its Kummer development. Cauchy's theorem gives a nontrivial cube
  root of unity, so cubing on the finite field is not injective and
  therefore not surjective. The degree bounds follow from the
  defining inequalities of the integer ceiling logarithm.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The binomial root supplies another noncube]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-binomial-extension}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.norm_root_binomialModulus,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.root_binomialModulus_noncube,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binomialModulus_root_properties}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-binomial-modulus}
  The root $t$ in $F[X]/(X^{3^s}-a)$ has norm $a$ over $F$.
  Consequently, if $a$ is a noncube, $t$ is also a noncube and
  $Z^{3^v}-t$ is monic and irreducible of degree exactly $3^v$.
  Both exponents may be zero. This supplies a second algebraic
  modulus without another coefficient-existence premise; an encoded
  field representation remains a separate construction.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Mathlib's power-basis norm formula gives $(-1)^{3^s}(-a)=a$.
  Applying the norm to a hypothetical equation $b^3=t$ contradicts
  the noncube premise on $a$. Apply the binomial irreducibility theorem
  in the first quotient field.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Sparse field and powering parameters]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-sparse-condenser-parameters}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sparseFieldExponent,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sparseFieldBits,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sparsePowerBits,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.condenserCoordinates}
  \leanok
  Set $s=\lceil\log_3\lceil (u+1)T/2\rceil\rceil$,
  $b=2\cdot3^s$, $r=b-T$, and $m=\lceil k/r\rceil$.
  The Lean definitions are total natural-number functions; the
  following bounds specify their positive-parameter behavior.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Sparse rounding preserves the output rate and error budget]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-sparse-condenser-parameters}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sparseFieldBits_lower,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sparseFieldBits_upper,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sparsePowerBits_add,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sparsePowerBits_pos,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sparsePowerBits_rate,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.condenserCoordinates_capacity,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.condenserCoordinates_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.condenserCoordinates_output,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.condenserCoordinates_loss,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sparseCondenser_output,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sparseCondenser_loss}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-sparse-condenser-parameters}
  For $u,T>0$ these choices satisfy
  \[
    (u+1)T\le b\le6(u+1)T,\qquad r>0,\qquad
    ub\le(u+1)r,\qquad k\le rm,\qquad m\le k.
  \]
  The seed has $b$ bits and the output has $mb$ bits, with
  $umb\le(u+1)k+ub$. If $Dk\le\epsilon2^T$, then
  \[
    \frac{(D-1)(2^r-1)m}{2^b}\le\epsilon.
  \]
  These are finite parameter inequalities, without an asymptotic or
  runtime assertion. Choosing the powering exponent after the sparse
  field size adapts Cheraghchi's condenser parameter tradeoff to the
  binomial degree grid; this choice is a deduction in this development.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Ceiling logarithms bound the rounded field width. Its lower bound
  gives $ub\le(u+1)(b-T)$. Ceiling division gives $(m-1)r\le k$,
